: We would like to thank I. Dumanski, E. Feigin, M. Finkelberg, G. Fourier and D. Kus for helpful email correspondences. We thank R. Travkin and X. Zhu for inspiring discussions on geometric aspects of this work. We also thank Wyatt Reeves for helpful discussions and sharing his unpublished results.
J. Hong was partially supported by the NSF grant DMS- 2001365.

2.
Affine Demazure modules of twisted current algebra

In this section, we collect some known results of affine Demazure modules, Weyl modules, and their twisted analogues, which will be used in Section
3
.

2.1.
Notations

Let
G
be an almost simple algebraic group over
\mathbb{C}
. We fix a maximal torus
T
and a Borel subgroup
B
containing
T
. Let
X^{*}(T)
(resp.
X_{*}(T)
) denote the weight lattice (resp. the coweight lattice) of
T
. Let
X_{*}(T)^{+}
denote the set of dominant coweights of
G
with respect to
B
. Let
R
be the set of roots of
G
. Let
R^{+}
denote the set of positive roots of
G
with respect to
B
. Let
\{\alpha_{i}\mid i\in I\}
be the set of simple roots, where
I
is the set of vertices of the Dynkin diagram of
G
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
,
\mathfrak{b}
,
\mathfrak{h}
denote the Lie algebras of
G
,
B
,
T
respectively. One has the triangular decomposition
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{n}^{-}\oplus\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{n}^{+}
, where
\mathfrak{n}^{+}
is the nilpotent radical of
\mathfrak{b}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{-}
is the nilpotent radical of the opposite Borel subalgebra
\mathfrak{b}^{-}
. Let
\{e_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in R\}
be a Chevalley basis of
\mathfrak{g}
. For simplicity of notations, we also set

e_{i}=e_{\alpha_{i}}
,
f_{i}=e_{-\alpha_{i}}
, and
h_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{\vee}
for any
i\in I
.

In this section, we always assume that
G
is simply-laced and of
adjoint type
. Then, the set
X_{*}(T)^{+}
can be identified with set of all dominant coweights of
\mathfrak{g}
. In Section
2
and Section
3
, we only deal with Lie algebras, but to be consistent with the notations in later sections on geometry, we keep using the notation
X_{*}(T)^{+}
.

2.2.
Twisted current algebra

Let
\tau
be a nontrivial diagram automorphism preserving
\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{h}
and the pinning
\{e_{i},f_{i};i\in I\}
. Let
m=2,3
or
4
and fix an
m
-th primitive root
\epsilon
.
Following
[
4
, Section 2.1]
, let
\sigma
be a standard automorphism of order
m
on
\mathfrak{g}
preserving
\mathfrak{b}
and
\mathfrak{h}
. More specifically, when
\mathfrak{g}
is not
A_{2\ell}
,
\sigma=\tau
; when
\mathfrak{g}
is of type
A_{2\ell}
,

(2.1)

\sigma=\tau\circ\epsilon^{h},

where
h\in\mathfrak{h}^{\tau}
such that

\alpha_{i}(h)=\begin{cases}0,&\text{if }i\neq\ell,\ell+1;\\
1,&\text{if }i=\ell,\ell+1.\end{cases}

In particular,

m=\begin{cases}2,&\text{if }\mathfrak{g}\text{ is }A_{2\ell-1},D_{\ell+1}\text{ or }E_{6};\\
3,&\text{if }\mathfrak{g}\text{ is }D_{4};\\
4,&\text{if }\mathfrak{g}\text{ is }A_{2\ell}.\end{cases}

Let
\mathfrak{g}^{\sigma}
be the
\sigma
-fixed point Lie subalgebra in
\mathfrak{g}
. We have the following descriptions of
\mathfrak{g^{\sigma}}
,

(2.2)

\begin{tabular}[]{|c | c | c |c |c |c | c| c| c|c |c|c|c|c|c |c ||}\hline\cr$(\mathfrak{g},m)$&$(A_{2\ell-1},2)$&$(A_{2\ell},4)$&$(D_{\ell+1},2)$&$(D_{4},3)$&$(E_{6},2)$\\[3.44444pt]
\hline\cr$\mathfrak{g}^{\sigma}$&$C_{\ell}$&$C_{\ell}$&$B_{\ell}$&$G_{2}$&$F_{4}$\\[3.44444pt]
\hline\cr\end{tabular},

where by convention
C_{1}
is
A_{1}
and
\ell\geq 3
for
D_{\ell+1}
.

We extend
\sigma
to an automorphism on the loop algebra
L(\mathfrak{g})=\mathfrak{g}((t))
by

(2.3)

\sigma(x\otimes t^{j})=\epsilon^{-j}\sigma(x)\otimes t^{j}.

We define the twisted affine algebra
\hat{L}(\mathfrak{g},\sigma):=\mathfrak{g}((t))^{\sigma}\oplus\mathbb{C}K
as a central extension of
L(\mathfrak{g})^{\sigma}
with the canonical center
K
as follows:

[x[f]+zK,x^{\prime}[f^{\prime}]+z^{\prime}K]=[x,x^{\prime}][ff^{\prime}]+m^{-1}\mathrm{Res}_{t=0}((df)f^{\prime})(x,x^{\prime})K

for
x[f],x^{\prime}[f^{\prime}]\in L(\mathfrak{g})
,
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}
, where
\mathrm{Res}_{t=0}
denotes the coefficient of
t^{-1}dt
, and
(,)
is the normalized Killing form on
\mathfrak{g}
such that
(\theta,\theta)=2
for the highest root
\theta
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
be the twisted current algebra, i.e. the
\sigma
-fixed point subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}[t]
. In fact,
\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
is a subalgebra of
\hat{L}(\mathfrak{g},\sigma)
.

(i)

When
\mathfrak{g}
is of type
A_{2\ell}
, let
\alpha_{i,j}
be the positive root
\alpha_{i}+\cdots+\alpha_{j}
, for
1\leq i\leq j\leq 2\ell
, and let
e_{\pm\alpha}
be the standard basis in
\mathfrak{g}=sl_{2\ell+1}
. Then
\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
has the following basis:

(2.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\quad&e_{\pm\alpha,2k}:=e_{\pm\alpha}\otimes t^{2k}+(-1)^{i+j+k}e_{\pm\tau(\alpha)}\otimes t^{2k},\text{ when }\alpha=\alpha_{i,j}\text{ with }1\leq i\leq j<\ell\\
&e_{\pm\alpha,2k}:=e_{\pm\alpha}\otimes t^{2k}-(-1)^{i+j+k}e_{\pm\tau(\alpha)}\otimes t^{2k},\text{ when }\alpha=\alpha_{i,2\ell-j}\text{ with }1\leq i\leq j<\ell\\
&e_{\pm\alpha,4k}:=e_{\pm\alpha}\otimes t^{4k},\text{ when }\alpha=\alpha_{i,2\ell+1-i}\text{ with }1\leq i\leq\ell\\
&e_{\pm\alpha,2k+1}:=e_{\pm\alpha}\otimes t^{2k+1}\pm(-1)^{i+\ell+k}e_{\pm\tau(\alpha)}\otimes t^{2k+1},\text{ when }\alpha=\alpha_{i,\ell}\text{ with }1\leq i\leq\ell\\
&h_{i,2k}:=h_{i}\otimes t^{2k}+(-1)^{k}h_{2\ell+1-i}\otimes t^{2k},\text{ for any }1\leq i\leq\ell\end{split}

(Note that the actions of
\tau
and
\sigma
agree on the roots.)

(ii)

When
\mathfrak{g}
is not of type
A_{2\ell}
, let
R^{+}_{s}
(resp.
R^{+}_{l}
) be the set of roots
\alpha\in R^{+}
such that the restriction
\alpha|_{\mathfrak{h}^{\sigma}}
is a short (resp. long) root of
\mathfrak{g}^{\sigma}
. Then
\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
has the following basis:

\displaystyle e_{\pm\alpha,k}:=\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}e_{\pm\sigma^{i}(\alpha)}\otimes(\epsilon^{i}t)^{k},\ \alpha\in R^{+}_{s};

\displaystyle e_{\pm\alpha,mk}:=\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}e_{\pm\sigma^{i}(\alpha)}\otimes t^{mk},\ \alpha\in R^{+}_{l};

\displaystyle h_{\alpha,k}:=\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}h_{\sigma^{i}(\alpha)}\otimes(\epsilon^{i}t)^{k},\ \alpha\in R^{+}.

When
\mathfrak{g}
is not
A_{2\ell}
,
\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
is isomorphic to the special twisted current algebra of
\hat{L}(\mathfrak{g},\sigma)
, see
[
27
, Section 4.2]
. When
\mathfrak{g}
is
A_{2\ell}
, we now show
\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
is isomorphic to the hyperspecial twisted current algebra
\mathfrak{Cg}
defined in
[
8
, Section 2.5]
and
[
27
, Section 1.5]
.

Assume that
\mathfrak{g}
is of type
A_{2\ell}
. Following
[
8
, Section 4.4]
and
[
27
, Section 1.8]
, the hyperspecial current algebra
\mathfrak{C}\mathfrak{g}\subset\mathfrak{g}[t,t^{-1}]^{\tau}
consists of the following basis elements:

(1)

e_{\pm\alpha}\otimes t^{k}+(-1)^{i+j}e_{\pm\tau(\alpha)}\otimes(-t)^{k}
,
\alpha=\alpha_{ij}
with
1\leq i\leq j<\ell
, and
k\geq 0
;

(2)

e_{\pm\alpha}\otimes t^{k\pm 1}+(-1)^{i+j}e_{\pm\tau(\alpha)}\otimes(-t)^{k\pm 1}
,
\alpha=\alpha_{i,2\ell-j}
with
1\leq i\leq j<\ell
, and
k\geq 0
;

(3)

e_{\pm\alpha}\otimes t^{2k\pm 1}
,
\alpha=\alpha_{i,2\ell+1-i}
with
1\leq i\leq\ell
, and
k\geq 0
;

(4)

e_{\pm\alpha}\otimes t^{(2k+1\pm 1)/2}+(-1)^{\ell+i}e_{\pm\tau(\alpha)}\otimes(-t)^{(2k+1\pm 1)/2}
,
\alpha=\alpha_{i\ell}
with
1\leq i\leq\ell
, and
k\geq 0
;

(5)

h_{i}\otimes t^{k}+h_{2\ell+1-i}\otimes(-t)^{k}
,
1\leq i\leq\ell
, and
k\geq 0
,

where
\alpha_{ij}
denotes the positive root
\alpha_{i}+\cdots+\alpha_{j}
, for any
1\leq i\leq j\leq 2\ell
. Note that
\tau(\alpha_{ij})=\alpha_{2\ell+1-j,2\ell+1-i}
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Assume that
\mathfrak{g}
is of type
A_{2\ell}
.
We have the following formula

(2.5)

\sigma(e_{\pm\alpha_{ij}})=(-1)^{j-i}\epsilon^{\pm\alpha_{ij}(h)}e_{\pm\alpha_{2\ell+1-j,2\ell+1-i}},\text{ for any }1\leq i\leq j\leq 2\ell+1,

and
\sigma(h_{i})=h_{2\ell+1-i}
, for any
1\leq i\leq 2\ell+1
, where
h
is defined in (
2.1
).

Proof.

In
[
25
, Section 7.10]
, the formula for the action of
\tau
on every basis vector in
\mathfrak{g}
is given. Combining this formula and
\sigma=\tau\epsilon^{h}
, we can deduce the formula for
\sigma
.
∎

Assume that
\mathfrak{g}
is of type
A_{2\ell}
. There exists an isomorphism
\eta_{k}:\hat{L}(\mathfrak{g},\tau)\simeq\hat{L}(\mathfrak{g},\sigma)
due to Kac (cf.
[
23
, p8]
and
[
25
, Section 8.5]
), which is defined as follows

\eta_{k}(x[t^{j}])=x[t^{2j+s}],

for any
x
a simultaneous
(-1)^{j}
-eigenvector of
\tau
, and a
s
-eigenvector of
\mathrm{ad}h
.
Let
\phi
be the Cartan involution on
\mathfrak{g}
such that
\phi(e_{i})=-f_{i}
and
\phi(h_{i})=-h_{i}
. Notice that for any root
\alpha
,
\phi(e_{\alpha})=-e_{-\alpha}
. Now, we define an automorphism
\eta_{c}:\hat{L}(\mathfrak{g},\tau)\to\hat{L}(\mathfrak{g},\tau)
as follows,

\eta_{c}(x[t^{s}])=\phi(x)[t^{s}],\text{ for any }x[t^{s}]\in\hat{L}(\mathfrak{g},\tau).

It is easy to verify that
\eta_{c}
is well-defined, since
\eta_{c}\circ\tau=\tau\circ\eta_{c}
, where
\tau
is the automorphism on the untwisted affine Lie algebra
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
induced from the action in (
2.3
) .
We define the following isomorphism of twisted affine algebras

(2.6)

\eta:=\eta_{k}\circ\eta_{c}:\hat{L}(\mathfrak{g},\tau)\simeq\hat{L}(\mathfrak{g},\sigma).

The following theorem originally appeared in an earlier version of
[
4
]
, where some calculation mistakes are fixed in this paper.

Theorem 2.2
.

The map
\eta
restricts to the following isomorphism of Lie algebras

\eta:\mathfrak{C}\mathfrak{g}\simeq\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}.

Proof.

From the definition (
2.6
), we have the following calculations:

(1)

When
\alpha=\alpha_{i}+\dots+\alpha_{j}
with
1\leq i\leq j<\ell
, and
k\geq 0
, we have

\eta(e_{\pm\alpha}\otimes t^{k}+(-1)^{i+j}e_{\pm\tau(\alpha)}\otimes(-t)^{k})=-(e_{\mp\alpha}\otimes t^{2k}+(-1)^{i+j+k}e_{\mp\tau(\alpha)}\otimes t^{2k}).

(2)

When
\alpha=\alpha_{i,2\ell-j}
with
1\leq i\leq j<\ell
, and
k\geq 0
, we have

\eta(e_{\pm\alpha}\otimes t^{k\pm 1}+(-1)^{i+j}e_{\pm\tau(\alpha)}\otimes(-t)^{k\pm 1})=-(e_{\mp\alpha}\otimes t^{2k}-(-1)^{i+j+k}e_{\mp\tau(\alpha)}\otimes t^{2k}).

(3)

When
\alpha=\alpha_{i,2\ell+1-i}
with
1\leq i\leq\ell
, and
k\geq 0
, we have

\eta(e_{\pm\alpha}\otimes t^{2k\pm 1})=-e_{\mp\alpha}\otimes t^{4k}.

(4)

When
\alpha=\alpha_{i\ell}
with
1\leq i\leq\ell
, and
k\geq 0
, we have

\eta(e_{\pm\alpha}\otimes t^{(2k+1\pm 1)/2}+(-1)^{\ell+i}e_{\pm\tau(\alpha)}\otimes(-t)^{(2k+1\pm 1)/2})=-(e_{\mp\alpha}\otimes t^{2k+1}\mp(-1)^{i+\ell+k}e_{\mp\tau(\alpha)}\otimes t^{2k+1}).

(5)

For any
1\leq i\leq\ell
, and
k\geq 0
, we have

\eta(h_{i}\otimes t^{k}+h_{2\ell+1-i}\otimes(-t)^{k})=-(h_{i}\otimes t^{2k}+(-1)^{k}h_{2\ell+1-i}\otimes t^{2k}).

Then, it is easy to see that the image of the described basis of the hyperspecial current algebra
\mathfrak{Cg}
under the map
\eta
is exactly the basis of
\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
described above.
∎

2.3.
Weyl modules

We denote by
P
(resp.
P_{\sigma}
) the weight lattice of
\mathfrak{g}
(resp.
\mathfrak{g}^{\sigma}
), and by
P^{+}
(resp.
P^{+}_{\sigma}
) the set of dominant weights of
\mathfrak{g}
(resp.
\mathfrak{g}^{\sigma}
) with respect to the Borel subalgebra
\mathfrak{b}
(resp.
\mathfrak{b}^{\sigma}
). We define the map
\iota:X_{*}(T)\to P
given by

\iota(\lambda)(h)=(\lambda,h),\ \forall\lambda\in X_{*}(T),h\in\mathfrak{h},

where
\lambda
is regarded as an element in
\mathfrak{h}
and
(\ ,\ )
is the normalized Killing form on
\mathfrak{h}
. By restricting
\iota(\lambda)
to
\mathfrak{h}^{\sigma}
, it induces a map
X_{*}(T)\to P_{\sigma}
, and it descends to the map
X_{*}(T)_{\sigma}\to P_{\sigma}
. We still denote it by
\iota
.

Following
[
20
]
, for any dominant weight
\mu\in P^{+}
of
\mathfrak{g}
, we define the Weyl module
W(\mu)
of
\mathfrak{g}[t]
to be the cyclic module generated by an element
w_{\mu}
subject to the following relations

\mathfrak{n}^{-}\otimes\mathbb{C}[t]\cdot w_{\mu}=0,\ \mathfrak{h}\otimes t\mathbb{C}[t]\cdot w_{\mu}=0,\ (h\otimes 1)\cdot w_{\mu}=-\mu(h)w_{\mu},\ (e_{\alpha_{i}}\otimes 1)^{\mu(\alpha_{i}^{\vee})+1}\cdot w_{\mu}=0,

for any simple roots
\alpha_{i}\in R^{+}
, and any
h\in\mathfrak{h}
.

Following
[
27
]
, for each dominant weight
\bar{\mu}\in P^{+}_{\sigma}
of
\mathfrak{g}^{\sigma}
, we define the twisted Weyl module
W^{\sigma}(\bar{\mu})
of
\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
to be the cyclic module generated by an element
w_{\bar{\mu}}
subject to the following relations

(2.7)

\mathfrak{n}^{-}[t]^{\sigma}\cdot w_{\bar{\mu}}=0,\ (\mathfrak{h}\otimes t\mathbb{C}[t])^{\sigma}\cdot w_{\bar{\mu}}=0,\ (h\otimes 1)\cdot v=-\bar{\mu}(h)w_{\bar{\mu}},\ (e_{\alpha,0})^{k_{\alpha}+1}\cdot w_{\bar{\mu}}=0,

for any
h\in\mathfrak{h}^{\sigma}
,
{\alpha}\in R^{+}
, and
k_{\alpha}=\bar{\mu}(\bar{\alpha}^{\vee})
, where
\bar{\alpha}
is the root
{\alpha}|_{\mathfrak{h}^{\sigma}}
of
\mathfrak{g}^{\sigma}
.

We define the twisted global Weyl module
\mathbb{W}^{\sigma}(\bar{\mu})
to be the cyclic
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma})
-module generated by an element
w_{\bar{\mu}}
with the following relations

\mathfrak{n}^{-}[t]^{\sigma}\cdot w_{\bar{\mu}}=0,\ (h\otimes 1)\cdot w_{\bar{\mu}}=-\bar{\mu}(h)w_{\bar{\mu}},\ (e_{\alpha,0})^{k_{\alpha}+1}\cdot w_{\bar{\mu}}=0,

where
h\in\mathfrak{h}^{\sigma}
,
{\alpha}\in R^{+}
, and
k_{\alpha}=\bar{\mu}(\bar{\alpha}^{\vee})
. Similar to the untwisted case in
[
7
]
, there is a well-defined right action of
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h}[t]^{\sigma})
on
\mathbb{W}^{\sigma}(\bar{\mu})
given by

(u\cdot w_{\bar{\mu}})\ h:=u\cdot(h\cdot w_{\bar{\mu}})

where
u\in\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}),h\in\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h}[t]^{\sigma})
. This action commutes with
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma})
-action. Let
{A}^{\sigma}(\bar{\mu}):=\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h}[t]^{\sigma})/\mathrm{Ann}(w_{\bar{\mu}})
be the quotient of
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h}[t]^{\sigma})
by the annihilator of
w_{\bar{\mu}}
.

Remark 2.3
.

In this paper, the (twisted) Weyl modules and the twisted global counterparts are defined using the lowest weight cyclic generators. By composing the map
\eta
(when
\mathfrak{g}
is
A_{2\ell}
) or a Cartan involution (otherwise), our definitions agree with the usual definitions in literature. Also see Remark
2.5
on the definitions of Demazure modules.

Let
I_{\sigma}
be the set of vertices of the Dynkin diagram of
\mathfrak{g}^{\sigma}
. We follow the Bourbaki labeling of the vertices of the Dynkin diagram, which gives a total order on
I
and
I_{\sigma}
. Let
\{\beta_{j}\}_{j\in I_{\sigma}}
be the set of simple roots of
\mathfrak{g}^{\sigma}
. Then, there is a map
\eta:I\to I_{\sigma}
such that

(2.8)

\check{\beta}_{i}=\sum_{j\in\eta^{-1}(i)}\check{\alpha}_{j}

for any simple coroot
\check{\beta}_{i}
, cf.
[
4
, Section 2.1]
. Let
\omega_{j}
be the fundamental weight of
\mathfrak{g}
associated to
j\in I
. For any
i\in I_{\sigma}
, let
\bar{\omega}_{i}:=\omega_{j}|_{\mathfrak{h}^{\sigma}}
for some
j\in\eta^{-1}(i)
. Then
\{\bar{\omega}_{i}\}_{i\in I_{\sigma}}
gives the set of fundamental weights of
\mathfrak{g}^{\sigma}
, cf.
[
4
, Section 2.1]
. Moreover, by
[
4
, Lemma 3.4]
, we have

(2.9)

\iota(\check{\omega}_{j})=\bar{\omega}_{i},\quad\forall j\in\eta^{-1}(i).

For each
i\in I_{\sigma}
, set
m_{i}:=\frac{m}{|\eta^{-1}(i)|}
. For example, when
\mathfrak{g}
is
A_{2\ell}
, we have
m_{i}=2
for any
i\in I_{\sigma}
. The following theorem on twisted global Weyl modules was proven in
[
8
]
, and it will be used in Section
3.3
.

Theorem 2.4
.

Let
\bar{\mu}=\sum_{i\in I_{\sigma}}n_{i}\bar{\omega}_{i}\in P^{+}_{\sigma}
.

(1)

The algebra
A^{\sigma}(\bar{\mu})
is a graded polynomial algebra in variables
T_{i,r}
of grade
m_{i}r
, where
i\in I_{\sigma}
and
1\leq r\leq n_{i}
. In particular, when
\mathfrak{g}
is
A_{2\ell}
,
m_{i}=2
for any
i\in I_{\sigma}
.

(2)

Let
I_{\bar{\mu},0}
be the unique graded maximal ideal in
A^{\sigma}(\bar{\mu})
. Then, there is a
\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
-isomorphism

\mathbb{W}^{\sigma}(\bar{\mu})\otimes_{A^{\sigma}(\bar{\mu})}(A^{\sigma}(\bar{\mu})/I_{\bar{\mu},0})\simeq W^{\sigma}(\bar{\mu}).

(3)

\mathbb{W}^{\sigma}(\bar{\mu})
is a free
A^{\sigma}(\bar{\mu})
-module of rank equal to

\prod_{i\in I_{\sigma}}\big(\dim W^{\sigma}(\bar{\omega}_{i})\big)^{n_{i}}
.

Proof.

For part (1), see
[
8
, Theorem 1]
when
\mathfrak{g}
is
A_{2\ell}
and
[
8
, Theorem 8]
when
\mathfrak{g}
is not
A_{2\ell}
. For part (2), it follows from the definition of twisted global Weyl module. For part (3), see
[
8
, Theorem 4, Theorem11]
.
∎

2.4.
Affine Demazure modules

The integrable highest weight modules of
\hat{L}(\mathfrak{g},\sigma)
of level
c
can be parameterized by a set
P(\sigma,c)
of certain highest weights of
\mathfrak{g}^{\sigma}
, see
[
23
, Section 2]
. In particular, for any
\kappa\in P(\sigma,1)
, we always have
c\kappa\in P(\sigma,c)
. We denote by
\mathscr{H}_{c}(\bar{\mu})
the integrable highest weight module associated to a highest weight
\bar{\mu}\in P(\sigma,c)
.

Set
\mathscr{H}_{c}=\oplus_{\kappa\in P(\sigma,1)}\mathscr{H}_{c}(c\kappa)
. For any
\bar{\lambda}\in X_{*}(T)_{\sigma}^{+}
, let
v_{\bar{\lambda}}\in\mathscr{H}_{c}
be the extremal weight vector whose
\mathfrak{h}^{\sigma}
-weight is
-c\iota(\bar{\lambda})
, see
[
4
, Lemma 3.6]
. We define the twisted affine Demazure
D^{\sigma}(c,\bar{\lambda})
as the following
\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
-module,

(2.10)

D^{\sigma}(c,\bar{\lambda}):=\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma})v_{\bar{\lambda}}\subseteq\mathscr{H}_{c}.

For any
\lambda\in X_{*}(T)^{+}
, the untwisted affine Demazure module
D(c,\lambda)
is defined in a similar way by simply taking
\sigma=\mathrm{id}
.

Remark 2.5
.

The affine Demazure module
D(c,\lambda)
defined here can be identified with the module
D(c,\lambda)
defined in
[
20
, Section 2.2]
with a
\mathfrak{g}[t]
-action via the Cartan involution
\eta_{c}:\mathfrak{g}[t]\to\mathfrak{g}[t]
. Similarly, our twisted affine Demazure module
D^{\sigma}(c,\bar{\lambda})
can be identified with the module
D(c,c\iota(\bar{\lambda}))
defined in
[
27
, Section 3.2]
via a twist of the Cartan involution
\eta_{c}:\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}\to\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
when
\mathfrak{g}
is not
A_{2\ell}
, or via a twist of the map
\eta:\mathfrak{Cg}\to\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
defined in (
2.6
) when
\mathfrak{g}
is of type
A_{2\ell}
.

Thanks to the works
[
20
,
27
]
of Fourier-Littelmann and Kus-Venkatesh, we have the following theorem, which will be used in the proof of Theorem
3.12
.

Theorem 2.6
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a simply-laced simple Lie algebra with a standard automorphism
\sigma
. Let
\lambda
be a dominant coweight of
\mathfrak{g}
. Denote by
\bar{\lambda}
the image of
\lambda
under the projection map
X_{*}(T)^{+}\to X_{*}(T)^{+}_{\sigma}
. Then

(1)

The untwisted Demazure module
D(c,\lambda)
is isomorphic to the quotient of the Weyl module
W(c\iota(\lambda))
by the submodule generated by

\{(e_{\alpha}\otimes t^{s})^{k_{\alpha,s}+1}\cdot w_{\lambda}\mid\alpha\in R^{+},s\geq 0\},

where
k_{\alpha,s}=c\cdot\max\{0,(\lambda,\alpha^{\vee})-s\}
, and
w_{\lambda}
is the generator whose
\mathfrak{h}
-weight is
-c\iota(\lambda)
.

(2)

The twisted Demazure module
D^{\sigma}(c,\bar{\lambda})
is isomorphic to the quotient of the twisted Weyl module
W^{\sigma}(c\iota(\bar{\lambda}))
by the submodule generated by

\displaystyle\{e_{\alpha,ms_{\alpha}}\cdot w_{\bar{\lambda}}\mid\alpha\in R^{+}_{l}\}\cup\{e_{\alpha,s_{\alpha}}\cdot w_{\bar{\lambda}}\mid\alpha\in R^{+}_{s}\},\text{ when }\mathfrak{g}\text{ is not }A_{2\ell};

\displaystyle\{e_{\alpha,4s_{\alpha}}\cdot w_{\bar{\lambda}}\mid\alpha=\alpha_{i,2\ell+1-i},1\leq i<\ell\}\cup\{e_{\alpha,2s_{2\alpha}+1}\cdot w_{\bar{\lambda}}\mid\alpha=\alpha_{i,\ell},1\leq i\leq\ell\}

\displaystyle\cup\{e_{\alpha,2s_{\alpha}}\cdot w_{\bar{\lambda}}\mid\alpha=\alpha_{i,j}\text{ or }\alpha_{i,2\ell-j},1\leq i<\ell\},\text{ when }\mathfrak{g}\text{ is }A_{2\ell},

where
s_{\alpha}=c\cdot(\lambda,\bar{\alpha}^{\vee})
, and
w_{\bar{\lambda}}
is the generator whose
\mathfrak{h}^{\sigma}
-weight is
-c\iota(\bar{\lambda})
. Here,
(,)
is the normalized Killing form on
\mathfrak{g}
and
\bar{\alpha}^{\vee}
is regarded as an element in
\mathfrak{h}^{\sigma}
.

Proof.

See
[
20
, Lemma 4, Corollary 1]
for part (1), and
[
27
, Theorem 3, Theorem 5]
for part (2).
∎

Corollary 2.7
.

With the same setup and assumption as in Theorem
2.6
, we have

(1)

For any
\lambda\in X_{*}(T)^{+}
, the Demazure module
D(1,\lambda)
is isomorphic to the Weyl module
W(\iota(\lambda))
.

(2)

For any
\bar{\lambda}\in X_{*}(T)_{\sigma}^{+}
, the twisted Demazure module
D^{\sigma}(1,\bar{\lambda})
is isomorphic to the twisted Weyl module
W^{\sigma}(\iota(\bar{\lambda}))
.

Proof.

For part (1), see
[
20
, Theorem 7]
. For part (2), see
[
15
, Theorem 5.1]
and
[
8
, Theorem 9(ii)]
when
\mathfrak{g}
is not type
A_{2\ell}
; see
[
8
, Theorem 2]
and
[
27
, Corollary 3.3]
when
\mathfrak{g}
is
A_{2\ell}
.
∎

Remark 2.8
.

When
\mathfrak{g}
is of type
A_{2\ell}
, part (2) of Corollary
2.7
does not hold for the diagram automorphism. This is one of the reasons that we use the ‘standard’ automorphism
\sigma
instead of the diagram automorphism.

In view of (
2.9
), this corollary implies that any Weyl module of
\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
is isomorphic to a twisted Demazure module.

3.
Global Demazure modules of twisted current algebra

In this section, we define twisted global Demazure modules and study their basic properties. We also prove that when the level is
1
, the global Demazure modules can be identified with twisted global Weyl modules.

3.1.
General twisted global modules

Let
V
be a cyclic graded finite dimensional
\mathfrak{g}[t]
-module with a cyclic vector
v_{\mu}
of weight
\mu\in P^{+}
such that

(3.1)

t\mathfrak{h}[t]\cdot v_{\mu}=0,

and the degree of
t
is
1
. For any
x\in\mathfrak{g}
and
s\geq 0
, we denote by
xt^{s}
the element
x\otimes t^{s}
in
\mathfrak{g}[t]
. We denote by
v[f]
the vector
v\otimes f
in the vector space
V[z]:=V\otimes\mathbb{C}[z]
, for any
v\in V
and
f\in\mathbb{C}[z]
. Following
[
19
, subsection 1.3]
, we define a
\mathfrak{g}[t]
-module structure on
V[z]
as follows,

(3.2)

xt^{s}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}v[f]=\sum_{j=0}^{s}\binom{s}{j}(xt^{j}\cdot v)[f\cdot z^{s-j}]

where
s\geq 0,x\in\mathfrak{g},v[f]\in V[z]
. It was shown in
[
19
, Section 1.3]
that
V[z]
is a cyclic module with the cyclic generator
v[1]
.

Consider the cyclic graded finite dimensional
\mathfrak{g}[t]
-modules
V_{1},\ldots,V_{n}
, where
V_{i}
has a cyclic vector
v_{i}
of weight
\mu_{i}
such that
t\mathfrak{h}[t]\cdot v_{i}=0.
Set

(3.3)

\textstyle w:=v_{1}[1]\otimes v_{2}[1]\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n}[1]\in\bigotimes_{i}V_{i}[z_{i}].

Definition 3.1
.

Similar to the untwisted case in
[
9
]
, we define the twisted global module
R^{\sigma}(V_{1},\ldots,V_{n})
to be a cyclic
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma})
-module generated by the vector
w
as follows,

R^{\sigma}(V_{1},\ldots,V_{n}):=\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma})\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}w\subseteq\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}[z_{i}].

Moreover, we define a right
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h}[t]^{\sigma})
-action on
R^{\sigma}(V_{1},\ldots,V_{n})
by

(3.4)

(u\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}w)\ h=u\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}(h\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}w),

where
u\in\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}),h\in\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h}[t]^{\sigma})
.

Lemma 3.2
.

This action (
3.4
) is well-defined.

Proof.

It suffices to show that if
u\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}w=0
for some
u\in\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma})
, then

u\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}(ht^{s}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}w)=0

for any
ht^{s}\in\mathfrak{h}[t]^{\sigma}
. It follows from the definition (
3.2
) with the following calculations:

(3.5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}u\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}(ht^{s}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}w)&=u\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}\big(ht^{s}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}(v_{1}[1]\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n}[1])\big)\\
&=u\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}v_{1}[1]\otimes\cdots\otimes(ht^{s}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}v_{i}[1])\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n}[1]\bigg)\\
&=u\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu_{i}(h)v_{1}[1]\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{i}[z_{i}^{s}]\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n}[1]\bigg)\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu_{i}(h)u\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}\big(v_{1}[1]\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{i}[z_{i}^{s}]\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n}[1]\big)\\
&=0\qed\end{split}

From the proof of Lemma
3.2
, we know the action (
3.4
) commutes with the
\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
-action, and only depends on the weights
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n}
. Set
\vec{\mu}=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n})
and we define

(3.6)

\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(\vec{\mu}):=\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h}[t]^{\sigma})/\mathrm{Ann}(w)

as the quotient of
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h}[t]^{\sigma})
by the annihilator of
w
defined in (
3.3
). Following
[
10
, Section 2.2]
, we call
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(\vec{\mu})
a twisted weight algebra associated to
w
. Then, the twisted global module
R^{\sigma}(V_{1},\ldots,V_{n})
is a
(\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma},\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(\vec{\mu}))
-bimodule.

Consider the following embedding

(3.7)

\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(\vec{\mu})\simeq\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(\vec{\mu})\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}w\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}[z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{n}],

given by
ht^{s}\mapsto\mu_{1}(h)z_{1}^{s}+\cdots+\mu_{n}(h)z_{n}^{s}
. Then,
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(\vec{\mu})
can be realized as the algebra generated by the following elements

\{\mu_{1}(h)z_{1}^{ms+j}+\mu_{2}(h)z_{2}^{ms+j}+\cdots+\mu_{n}(h)z_{n}^{ms+j}\mid h\in\mathfrak{h}_{j},0\leq j\leq m-1,s\geq 0\},

where
\mathfrak{h}_{j}:=\{h\in\mathfrak{h}\mid\sigma(h)=\epsilon^{j}h\}
.

Example 3.3
.

Let
v
be a vector of weight
\omega_{j}
, where
j\in I
. For any
ht^{s}\in\mathfrak{h}[t]^{\sigma}
,
ht^{s}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}v[1]=\omega_{j}(h)v[z^{s}]
is non-zero if and only if
m_{j}\mid s
, where
m_{j}
is the order of the stablizer of the vertex
j
under the action of the cyclic group
\langle\sigma\rangle
. Thus,
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(\omega_{j})\simeq\mathbb{C}[z^{m_{j}}]
. In particular, when
\mathfrak{g}
is
A_{2\ell}
,
m_{j}=2
for any
j
.

Similar to the untwisted case in
[
9
]
, we have the following result.

Proposition 3.4
.

\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(\vec{\mu})
is a Noetherian ring.

Proof.

Consider an element
h\in\mathfrak{h}^{\sigma}
such that
(\mu_{i_{1}}+\cdots+\mu_{i_{k}})(h)\neq 0
for any subset
\{i_{1},\ldots i_{k}\}\subset\{1,\ldots,n\}
. Let
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(\vec{\mu})_{h}
be the subalgebra of
\mathbb{C}[z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{n}]
generated by

\{\mu_{1}(h)z_{1}^{ms}+\mu_{2}(h)z_{2}^{ms}+\cdots+\mu_{n}(h)z_{n}^{ms}\mid s\geq 0\}.

Then we have the following embeddings

\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(\vec{\mu})_{h}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(\vec{\mu})\hookrightarrow\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(\mu_{i}).

It was shown in
[
2
, Proposition 2.6]
that
\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(\mu_{i})
is finite over
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(\vec{\mu})_{h}
, and hence is finite over
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(\vec{\mu})
. Note that
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(\vec{\mu})_{h}
is Noetherian. By Artin-Tate Lemma
[
1
, Proposition 7.8]
,
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(\vec{\mu})
is finitely generated over
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(\vec{\mu})_{h}
. Thus,
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(\vec{\mu})
is also Noetherian.
∎

Remark 3.5
.

The definitions and results for
R^{\sigma}(V_{1},\ldots,V_{n})
and
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(\vec{\mu})
in this section also works when every
\mu_{i}
is anti-dominant.

For a point
p\in\mathbb{A}^{1}-\{0\}
and a
\mathfrak{g}[t]
-module
V
, we denote by
V_{p}
the module
V
with a new
\mathfrak{g}[t]
-action with a shift by
p
, i.e. it is given by

(3.8)

xt^{s}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}v:=x(t+p)^{s}\cdot v,

where
xt^{s}\in\mathfrak{g}[t]
,
v\in V
.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
V_{1}
be a cyclic finite dimensional graded
\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
-module,
V_{2},\ldots,V_{n}
be cyclic finite dimensional graded
\mathfrak{g}[t]
-modules. Let
\vec{p}\in\mathbb{A}^{n}
such that
p_{1}=0,p_{i}^{m}\neq p_{j}^{m}
for any
i\neq j
. Then, the tensor product
V_{1}\otimes\bigotimes_{i=2}^{n}V_{i,{p_{i}}}
is a cyclic
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma})
-module.

Proof.

See
[
27
, Section 6.3]
. One can also prove this proposition by using a similar method in
[
17
, Proposition 1.4]
.
∎

When
V_{1}
is a trivial module, we get the following special case.

Corollary 3.7
.

Let
\vec{p}\in\mathbb{A}^{n}
such that
p_{i}^{m}\neq p_{j}^{m}\neq 0
for any
i\neq j
. The tensor product
\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}V_{i,p_{i}}
is a cyclic
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma})
-module. ∎

The
\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma})
-module
\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}V_{i,{p_{i}}}
is cyclic, and hence it has a filtration induced from the
t
-grading on
\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
. Let
\mathrm{gr}\,\big(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}V_{i,{p_{i}}}\big)
denote the associated graded
\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
-module of
\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}V_{i,{p_{i}}}
.

Proposition 3.8
.

Let
\vec{p}\in\mathbb{A}^{n}
such that
p_{i}^{m}\neq p_{j}^{m}\neq 0
for any
i\neq j
. For each
1\leq i\leq n
, let
V_{i}
be a cyclic graded finite dimensional
\mathfrak{g}[t]
-module with a cyclic vector
v_{i}
of weight
\mu_{i}\in P^{+}
such that
t\mathfrak{h}[t]\cdot v_{i}=0
. There is a surjective morphism of
\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
-modules

(3.9)

R^{\sigma}(V_{1},\ldots,V_{n})\otimes_{\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(\vec{\mu})}\mathbb{C}_{\vec{p}}\twoheadrightarrow\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}V_{i,{p_{i}}}.

Proof.

Consider the composition of morphisms of
\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
-modules

R^{\sigma}(V_{1},\ldots,V_{n})\otimes_{\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(\vec{\mu})}\mathbb{C}_{\vec{p}}\to\bigg(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}[z_{i}]\bigg)\otimes_{\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(\vec{\mu})}\mathbb{C}_{\vec{p}}\twoheadrightarrow\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}V_{i,{p_{i}}}.

The proposition follows from Corollary
3.7
which asserts that the right hand side is a cyclic
\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
-module.
∎

We will show in Theorem
3.13
that the map (
3.9
) is an isomorphism when
V_{i}
are Demazure modules. The following theorem is a special case of
[
27
, Theorem 7]
, which will be crucially used in Section
3.2
.

Theorem 3.9
.

Let
\vec{p}\in\mathbb{A}^{n}
such that
p_{i}^{m}\neq p_{j}^{m}\neq 0
for any
i\neq j
. Given
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\in X_{*}(T)^{+}
, let
\bar{\lambda}
be the image of
\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}
in
X_{*}(T)_{\sigma}
. Then, there is an isomorphism of
\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
-modules

D^{\sigma}(c,\bar{\lambda})\simeq\mathrm{gr}\,\bigg(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}D(c,\lambda_{i})_{p_{i}}\bigg).\qed

3.2.
Twisted global Demazure modules

In this subsection, we define the global Demazure modules of twisted current algebras, and study their basic properties.

Given
\vec{\lambda}=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n})\in(X_{*}(T)^{+})^{n}
, for each
i
, let
v_{i}
be the cyclic generator of the Demazure module
D(c,\lambda_{i})
of
\mathfrak{g}[t]
which is of weight
-c\iota(\lambda_{i})
, as in
(\ref{eq_Demazure})
. Recall Section
3.1
, one can define the following twisted global module

R^{\sigma}\big(D(c,\lambda_{1}),\ldots,D(c,\lambda_{n})\big)=\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma})\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}w,

where
w=v_{1}[1]\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n}[1]\in\bigotimes D(c,\lambda_{i})[z_{i}]
. Moreover, this global module has a right
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})
-action, where
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})=\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h}[t]^{\sigma})/\mathrm{Ann}(w)
is the twisted weight algebra associated to
w
. We remark that
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})=\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(-c\iota(\vec{\lambda}))
, see (
3.6
). By (
3.7
), we have an embedding
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{A}^{n}]:=\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}]

Definition 3.10
.

The twisted global Demazure module
\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})
is defined to be the following
(\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma},\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda}))
-bimodule

(3.10)

\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda}):=R^{\sigma}\big(D(c,\lambda_{1}),\ldots,D(c,\lambda_{n})\big).

Let
\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})_{\vec{p}}:=\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})\otimes_{\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})}\mathbb{C}_{\vec{p}}
denote the fiber at a point
\vec{p}\in\mathbb{A}^{n}
.

We now determine the generic fibers of the twisted global Demazure module.

Lemma 3.11
.

With the same setup as in Theorem
3.9
, there is a surjective
\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
-morphism

(3.11)

\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})_{\vec{p}}\twoheadrightarrow\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}D(c,\lambda_{i})_{p_{i}}.

As a consequence, there is an inequality

(3.12)

\dim\big(\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})_{\vec{p}}\big)\geq\dim\bigg(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}D(c,\lambda_{i})_{p_{i}}\bigg)=\dim\big(D^{\sigma}(c,\bar{\lambda})\big).

Proof.

The surjective map is given by Proposition
3.8
. The inequality follows from the surjection (
3.11
) and Theorem
3.9
.
∎

Theorem 3.12
.

Given
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\in X_{*}(T)^{+}
, let
\bar{\lambda}
be the image of
\lambda:=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}
in
X_{*}(T)^{+}_{\sigma}
. There is a
\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
-isomorphism
\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})_{0}\simeq D^{\sigma}(c,\bar{\lambda})
.

Our proof is similar to the proofs in
[
10
, Proposition 4.9]
and
[
9
, Proposition 2.13]
.

Proof.

It is known that the function
\varphi(\vec{p}):=\dim\big(\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})_{\vec{p}}\big)
is upper semicontinuous, cf.
[
21
, Expample 12.7.2]
. Note that for any
\vec{p}\neq 0
, the function
\varphi
is a constant on the punctured line
\{a\vec{p}\mid a\in\mathbb{C}^{*}\}
. Thus,

(3.13)

\varphi({0})\geq\varphi(\vec{p}),\text{ for any }\vec{p}\in\mathbb{A}^{n}.

Combining with (
3.12
), we get

(3.14)

\dim\big(\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})_{0}\big)\geq\dim\big(D^{\sigma}(c,\bar{\lambda})\big).

From Theorem
2.6
,
D^{\sigma}(c,\bar{\lambda})
is isomorphic to the quotient of twisted Weyl module
W^{\sigma}(c\iota(\bar{\lambda}))
by a submodule
M
, where
M
is generated by the elements in Theorem
2.6
part (2). To prove the theorem, it suffices to show there is a surjective map

D^{\sigma}(c,\bar{\lambda})\mathrel{\!\tilde{\,\to}}W^{\sigma}(c\iota(\bar{\lambda}))/M\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})_{0}.

We first show that the map

(3.15)

\psi:W^{\sigma}(c\iota(\bar{\lambda}))\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})\otimes_{\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})}\mathbb{C}_{0}

sending
w_{\bar{\lambda}}
to
w\otimes 1
is well-defined. It is clear that the first three defining relations in (
2.7
) for
w_{\bar{\lambda}}
also holds for
w\otimes 1
. The reason is that
w=v_{1}[1]\otimes v_{2}[1]\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n}[1]
and by part (1) of Theorem
2.6

v_{i}
satisfies the following relations

(3.16)

\mathfrak{n}^{-}[t]\cdot v_{i}=0,\ \mathfrak{h}\otimes t\mathbb{C}[t]\cdot v_{i}=0,\ (h\otimes 1)\cdot v=-c(\lambda_{i},h)v_{i},\ (e_{\alpha}\otimes t^{s})^{k_{\alpha,s}+1}\cdot v_{i}=0,

where
\alpha\in R^{+}
,
k_{\alpha,s}=c\max\{0,(\lambda,\alpha^{\vee})-s\}
.

We now check the last relation in (
2.7
). When
\mathfrak{g}
is not
A_{2\ell}
, for any
\alpha\in R^{+}
, if
\alpha
is not fixed by
\sigma
, then the roots
\alpha,\sigma(\alpha),\ldots,\sigma^{m-1}(\alpha)
are all distinct and orthogonal, cf.
[
34
, Lemma 1]
. In this case, we have
k_{\alpha}=c(\lambda,\bar{\alpha}^{\vee})\geq c\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}({\lambda},\ {\sigma^{j}(\alpha^{\vee})})
, and

(3.17)

(e_{\alpha,0})^{k_{\alpha}+1}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}w=\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}e_{\sigma^{j}(\alpha)}\otimes 1\bigg)^{k_{\alpha}+1}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}w=\sum_{\sum s_{j}=k_{\alpha}+1}\prod_{j=0}^{m-1}(e_{\sigma^{j}(\alpha)}\otimes 1)^{s_{j}}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}w.

In each term of the right side, there must be an index
j
such that
s_{j}\geq c(\lambda,\sigma^{j}(\alpha^{\vee}))+1=k_{\sigma^{j}(\alpha),0}+1
. For this
s_{j}
, by the last relation in (
3.16
), we have
(e_{\sigma^{j}(\alpha)}\otimes 1)^{s_{j}}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}(v_{i}[1])=0
,
1\leq i\leq n
. This shows that the right side of (
3.17
) is
0
. Hence, the last relation in (
2.7
) holds in this case. When
\alpha
is fixed by
\sigma
, this relation can be checked similarly (actually easier).

When
\mathfrak{g}
is of type
A_{2\ell}
and
m=4
, the element
e_{\alpha,0}\in\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
if and only if
\alpha\neq\alpha_{i,\ell}
, see Section
2.2
. Thus,
\alpha
and
\sigma(\alpha)
are either equal or orthogonal. By the same argument as above, we still have
(e_{{\alpha},0})^{k_{\alpha}+1}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}w=0
, where
\alpha\neq\alpha_{i,\ell}
and
k_{\alpha}=c(\lambda,\bar{\alpha}^{\vee})
. This finishes the checking of all relations in (
2.7
). Thus, the map (
3.15
) is well-defined.

To complete the proof, we still need to show
\psi(M)=0
. When
\mathfrak{g}
is not
A_{2\ell}
, the submodule
M
is generated by the following elements (see Theorem
2.6
)

\{e_{\alpha,ms_{\alpha}}\cdot w_{\bar{\lambda}}\mid\alpha\in R^{+}_{l}\}\cup\{e_{\alpha,s_{\alpha}}\cdot w_{\bar{\lambda}}\mid\alpha\in R^{+}_{s}\}.

By the equalities in
[
25
, (5.1.1)]
and (
2.8
), we always have
s_{\alpha}=c(\lambda,\bar{\alpha}^{\vee})\geq c(\lambda,\sigma^{j}(\alpha^{\vee}))
for any
\alpha\in R^{+}
and
0\leq j\leq m-1
. When
\alpha\in R^{+}_{l}
, by definition
\bar{\alpha}
is a long root of
\mathfrak{g}^{\sigma}
. Then,
(\lambda,\sigma^{j}(\alpha^{\vee}))-ms_{\alpha}\leq(\lambda,\sigma^{j}(\alpha^{\vee}))-mc({\lambda},\ {\sigma^{j}(\alpha^{\vee})})\leq 0
, which implies

(3.18)

e_{\alpha,ms_{\alpha}}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}w=\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}e_{\sigma^{i}(\alpha)}\otimes t^{ms_{\alpha}}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}w=0.

Similarly, one can show that
e_{\alpha,s_{\alpha}}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}w=0
for any
\alpha\in R^{+}_{s}
. Thus,
\psi(M)=0
.

When
\mathfrak{g}
is of type
A_{2\ell}
, the submodule
M
is generated by the following elements

\displaystyle\{e_{\alpha,4s_{\alpha}}\cdot w_{\bar{\lambda}}\mid\alpha=\alpha_{i,2\ell+1-i},1\leq i<\ell\}\cup\{e_{\alpha,2s_{2\alpha}+1}\cdot w_{\bar{\lambda}}\mid\alpha=\alpha_{i,\ell},1\leq i\leq\ell\}

\displaystyle\cup\{e_{\alpha,2s_{\alpha}}\cdot w_{\bar{\lambda}}\mid\alpha=\alpha_{i,j}\text{ or }\alpha_{i,2\ell-j},1\leq i<\ell\}.

When
\alpha\neq\alpha_{i,\ell}
, the relations can be checked similarly as in (
3.18
). Now, suppose
\alpha=\alpha_{i,\ell}
, and let
s=s_{2\alpha}=c(\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}+\tau(\alpha^{\vee}))
. We have

(e_{\alpha,2s+1})\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}w=(e_{\alpha}\otimes t^{2s+1}+(-1)^{i+\ell+s}e_{\tau(\alpha)}\otimes t^{2s+1})\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}w=0,

since
(\lambda,\tau^{p}(\alpha^{\vee}))-(2s+1)=(\lambda,\tau^{p}(\alpha^{\vee}))-2c(\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}+\tau(\alpha^{\vee}))-1<0
for
p=0
, or
1
. Thus, in this case we also have
\psi(M)=0
.

Now, the map
\psi
induces a surjective morphism

\bar{\psi}:D^{\sigma}(c,\bar{\lambda})\mathrel{\!\tilde{\,\to}}W^{\sigma}(c\iota(\bar{\lambda}))/M\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})_{0}.

By the dimension inequality (
3.14
),
\bar{\psi}
must be an isomorphism.
∎

Theorem 3.13
.

Given
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\in X_{*}(T)^{+}
, let
\bar{\lambda}
be the image of
\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}
in
X_{*}(T)^{+}_{\sigma}
.

(1)

For any
\vec{p}\in\mathbb{A}^{n}
such that
p_{i}^{m}\neq p_{j}^{m}\neq 0
for any
i\neq j
, there is an isomorphism of
\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
-modules

\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})_{\vec{p}}\simeq\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}D(c,\lambda_{i})_{p_{i}}.

(2)

The twisted global Demazure module
\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})
is free over
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})
.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.12
, we have
\dim\big(\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})_{0}\big)=\dim\big(D^{\sigma}(c,\bar{\lambda})\big)
. Combining with (
3.13
), we get

(3.19)

\dim\big(D^{\sigma}(c,\bar{\lambda})\big)\geq\dim\big(\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})_{\vec{p}}\big)

for any
\vec{p}\in\mathbb{A}^{n}
. It follows that all three terms in the inequality (
3.12
) are equal. Hence the surjection (
3.11
) is an isomorphism. This proves part (1).

From part (1), we get
\dim\big(\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})_{\vec{p}}\big)=\dim\big(D^{\sigma}(c,\bar{\lambda})\big)
for any
\vec{p}
such that
p_{i}^{m}\neq p_{j}^{m}\neq 0,\forall i\neq j
. By the semicontinuity of the function
\varphi(\vec{p}):=\dim\big(\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})_{\vec{p}}\big)
, we get

\dim\big(\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})_{\vec{p}}\big)\geq\dim\big(D^{\sigma}(c,\bar{\lambda})\big)

for any
\vec{p}\in\mathbb{A}^{n}
. Combining with the inequality (
3.19
), the function
\varphi(\vec{p})
must be constant. Thus,
\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})
is a graded projective
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})
-module. By the graded Nakayama lemma (cf.
[
35
,
Tag 0EKB
]
),
\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})
is a free
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})
-module.
∎

Remark 3.14
.

In Section
6
, we will use the geometry of Beilinson-Drinfeld Schubert varieties to fully describe the fibers of
\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})
at any point in
\mathbb{A}^{n}
, see Corollary
6.7
.

We now introduce some notations that will be frequently used throughout this paper.

Definition 3.15
.

Denote by
[n]
the finite set
\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
. Let
\xi=(I_{1},I_{2},\ldots,I_{k})
be a partition of
[n]
. We define
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\xi}
to be the following open subset in
\mathbb{A}^{n}
,

(3.20)

\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\xi}=\{\vec{p}\in\mathbb{A}^{n}\mid{p}_{i}^{m}\neq{p}_{j}^{m},\ \forall i\in I_{\alpha},j\in I_{\beta},\alpha\neq\beta\}.

For any subset
I=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{s}\}\subseteq[n]
, set
\mathbb{A}^{I}={\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{C}[z_{i_{1}},\ldots,z_{i_{s}}]
.

Remark 3.16
.

In this paper, the partition
\xi=(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})
is always ordered by lexicographical order, i.e.
\min(I_{1})<\min(I_{2})<\cdots<\min(I_{k})
.

Example 3.17
.

When
\xi=([n])
is the trivial partition of
[n]
, we have
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\xi}=\mathbb{A}^{n}
; when
\xi_{0}=(\{1\},\{2\},\ldots,\{n\})
is the finest partition, we have
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\xi_{0}}=\{\vec{p}\in\mathbb{A}^{n}\mid\bar{p}_{i}\neq\bar{p}_{j},\ \forall i\neq j\}
.

Given a partition
\xi=(I_{1},I_{2},\ldots,I_{k})
of
[n]
, for any
1\leq j\leq k
, we label elements in
I_{j}=\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}\}
by the natural order of integers, i.e.
j_{1}<j_{2}<\cdots<j_{s}
, where
s
is the cardinality of
I_{j}
.
For any point
\vec{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})\in\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\xi}
and
\vec{\lambda}\in(X_{*}(T)^{+})^{n}
, we make the following notations:

(3.21)

\displaystyle\vec{p}_{I_{j}}=(p_{j_{1}},\ldots,p_{j_{s}})\in\mathbb{A}^{I_{j}},

(3.22)

\displaystyle\vec{\lambda}_{I_{j}}=(\lambda_{j_{1}},\ldots,\lambda_{j_{s}}),

\displaystyle w_{I_{j}}=v_{j_{1}}[1]\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{j_{s}}[1]\in\bigotimes_{i=1}^{s}D(c,\lambda_{j_{i}})[z_{j_{i}}].

For simplicity, we denote by
w_{I_{j}}[1]
the generator
w_{I_{j}}\otimes 1
of

\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda}_{I_{j}})_{\mathbb{A}^{I_{j}}}:=\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda}_{I_{j}})\otimes_{\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda}_{I_{j}})}\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{A}^{I_{j}}].

Proposition 3.18
.

(1)

For any
\lambda\in X_{*}(T)^{+}
, there is an embedding of
\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
-modules:

\phi:\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\lambda)_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\hookrightarrow D(c,\lambda)[z]

defined by
(u\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}w)[f]\mapsto u\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}(v[f])
for any
u\in\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
and
f\in\mathbb{C}[z]
, where
w:=v[1]
and
v
is the cyclic generator of
D(c,\lambda)
as in (
2.10
).

(2)

Let
\xi=(I,J)
be a partition of
[n]
. There is an embedding of
\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
-modules:

(3.23)

\psi_{\xi}:\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})_{\mathbb{A}^{n}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{A}^{n}]}\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\xi}]\hookrightarrow\big(\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda}_{I})_{\mathbb{A}^{I}}\otimes\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda}_{J})_{\mathbb{A}^{J}}\big)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{A}^{I}\times\mathbb{A}^{J}]}\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\xi}]

defined by
(u\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}w)[f]\otimes g\mapsto\big((u\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}w_{I})[1]\otimes(u\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}w_{J})[1]\big)\otimes(fg)
for any
u\in\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
,
f\in\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{A}^{n}]
, and
g\in\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\xi}]
.

Proof.

(1) By Theorem
3.13
, the map
\phi
restricts to an isomorphism
\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\lambda)_{p}\simeq D(c,\lambda)_{p}
at any
p\in\mathbb{A}^{1}\backslash\{0\}
. Thus,
\phi
is injective.

(2) The map
\psi_{\xi}
is well defined since
u
acts on
w
via Leibniz rule. Again, by Theorem
3.13
, it restricts to an isomorphism

\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})_{\vec{p}}\simeq\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda}_{I})_{\vec{p}_{I}}\otimes\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda}_{J})_{\vec{p}_{J}},

for any
\vec{p}
such that
p_{i}^{m}\neq p_{j}^{m}\neq 0,\forall i\neq j
. It follows that the map
\psi_{\xi}
is injective.
∎

Remark 3.19
.

In Section
6
, we will show that the injective map
\psi_{\xi}
is actually an isomorphism, see Theorem
6.5
.

3.3.
Twisted global Weyl modules

In this subsection, we identify the twisted global Weyl module defined in Section
2.3
with the level one twisted global Demazure module.

Taking
c=1
in Theorem
3.12
and Theorem
3.13
and applying Corollary
2.7
, we get the following results.

Lemma 3.20
.

Given a tuple of weights
\vec{\mu}=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n})\in(P^{+})^{n}
, let
W(\mu_{i})
be the local Weyl module defined in Section
2.3
. Then

(1)

The global module
R^{\sigma}(W(\mu_{1}),\ldots,W(\mu_{n}))
is a graded free
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(-\vec{\mu})
-module.

(2)

Let
\bar{\mu}\in P_{\sigma}^{+}
be the restriction of
\sum\mu_{i}
to
\mathfrak{h}^{\sigma}
. There is a
\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
-isomorphism

R^{\sigma}(W(\mu_{1}),\ldots,W(\mu_{n}))\otimes_{\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(-\vec{\mu})}\mathbb{C}_{0}\simeq W^{\sigma}(\bar{\mu}).\qed

Proposition 3.21
.

Given any fundamental weights
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n}\in P^{+}
, let
\bar{\mu}\in P_{\sigma}^{+}
be the restriction of
\sum\mu_{i}
to
\mathfrak{h}^{\sigma}
. Then, there is a
\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
-isomorphism

\mathbb{W}^{\sigma}(\bar{\mu})\simeq R^{\sigma}(W(\mu_{1}),\ldots,W(\mu_{n})),

and an isomorphism
A^{\sigma}(\bar{\mu})\simeq\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(-\vec{\mu})
of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras, where
\vec{\mu}=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n})
.

Proof.

Let
w_{i}\in W(\mu_{i})
(resp.
w_{\bar{\mu}}\in\mathbb{W}^{\sigma}(\bar{\mu})
) denote the cyclic generator with weight
-\mu_{i}
(resp.
-\bar{\mu}
). Similar to the map (
3.15
), we have a surjective
\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
-morphism

(3.24)

\mathbb{W}^{\sigma}(\bar{\mu})\twoheadrightarrow R^{\sigma}(W(\mu_{1}),\ldots,W(\mu_{n})),

given by
w_{\bar{\mu}}\mapsto w:=w_{1}[1]\otimes\cdots\otimes w_{n}[1]
. This induces a surjective map

(3.25)

A^{\sigma}(\bar{\mu})=\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h}[t]^{\sigma})/\mathrm{Ann}(w_{\bar{\mu}})\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h}[t]^{\sigma})/\mathrm{Ann}(w)=\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(-\vec{\mu}).

We claim this is an isomorphism. We only prove the case when
\mathfrak{g}
is of type
A_{2\ell}
. The other types can be checked similarly.

Recall Theorem
2.4
that
A^{\sigma}(\bar{\mu})
is a a graded polynomial algebra in variables
T_{i,r}
of grade
2r
, where
1\leq i\leq\ell
and
1\leq r\leq\bar{\mu}(\beta_{i}^{\vee})
. Set
n_{i}:=\bar{\mu}(\beta_{i}^{\vee})
. Then,
n=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}n_{i}
. In view of
[
8
, Section 5.2 and 5.3]
,
T_{i,r}
is the image of
P_{i,r}\in\mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{h}[t]^{\sigma})
which is defined as follows. For any
1\leq i\leq\ell
, set
P_{i,1}:=h_{i,2}
, which is defined in (
2.4
). For
r\geq 2
, we inductively define

P_{i,r}:=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{s=0}^{r}h_{i,2s+2}P_{i,r-s-1}.

Now, we determine the image of
T_{i,r}
under the map (
3.25
) by examining the action of
P_{i,r}
on
w
, where
1\leq i\leq\ell
and
1\leq r\leq n_{i}
. Suppose
n_{i}\geq 1
, otherwise
P_{i,r}
will not appear. From
[
8
, Section 5.4]
, we have

(3.26)

P_{i,r}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}w=\sum_{j_{1}+\cdots+j_{n}=r}\big(P_{i,j_{1}}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}w_{1}[1]\big)\otimes\cdots\otimes\big(P_{i,j_{n}}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}w_{n}[1]\big),

where
j_{k}\geq 0
and by convention
P_{i,0}=1
. Observe that
h_{i,2s+2}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}w_{k}[1]=\mu_{k}(h_{i}+(-1)^{s+1}h_{2\ell+1-i})w_{k}[z_{k}^{2s+2}]
is nonzero only when
\mu_{k}=\omega_{i}\text{ or }\omega_{2\ell+1-i}
. Without loss of generality, suppose
\mu_{k}=\omega_{i}
for
1\leq k\leq n_{i}^{\prime}
, and
\mu=\omega_{2\ell+1-i}
for
n_{i}^{\prime}+1\leq k\leq n_{i}
, where
n_{i}^{\prime}
is the number of
\omega_{i}
appearing in
\vec{\mu}
. Then,
P_{i,j_{k}}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}w_{k}[1]=0
whenever
j_{k}>1
, or
k>r
and
j_{k}\geq 1
. Thus, the equation (
3.26
) becomes

(3.27)

P_{i,r}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{\textbullet}}}w=\sum_{j_{1}+\cdots+j_{n_{i}}=r}(-1)^{a}\big(w_{1}[z_{1}^{2j_{1}}]\otimes\cdots\otimes w_{n_{i}}[z_{n_{i}}^{2j_{n_{i}}}]\otimes w_{n_{i}+1}[1]\otimes\cdots\otimes w_{n}[1]\big),

where
0\leq j_{k}\leq 1
and
a=j_{n_{i}^{\prime}+1}+\cdots+j_{n_{i}}
.

Recall the embedding
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(-\vec{\mu})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}]
defined in (
3.7
). Composing it with the map (
3.25
), we get a morphism

(3.28)

A^{\sigma}(\bar{\mu})\to\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}].

Given
1\leq i\leq\ell
, we define
K_{i}=\{1\leq k\leq n\mid\mu_{k}=\omega_{i}\text{ or }\omega_{2\ell+1-i}\}
, which is of cardinality
n_{i}
. The symmetric group
S_{n_{i}}
permutes
K_{i}
. Let
S
be the Young subgroup
S_{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times S_{n_{\ell}}
of
S_{n}
. For any
1\leq k\leq n
, set
\delta_{k}=1
if
\mu_{k}=\omega_{i}
;
\delta_{k}=\sqrt{-1}
if
\mu_{k}=\omega_{2\ell+1-i}
. We define the following polynomial

f_{i,r}:=\sum_{k\in K_{i},\ {\scriptscriptstyle\sum}j_{k}=r}(\delta_{k}z_{k})^{2j_{k}}\in\mathbb{C}\big[(\delta_{1}z_{1})^{2},\ldots,(\delta_{n}z_{n})^{2}\big]^{S}.

From the computation (
3.27
), the map (
3.28
) sends
T_{i,r}
to
f_{i,r}
. Note that
\{f_{i,r}\mid 1\leq i\leq\ell,1\leq r\leq n_{i}\}
is exactly a set of algebraically independent generators of the algebra
\mathbb{C}\big[(\delta_{1}z_{1})^{2},\ldots,(\delta_{n}z_{n})^{2}\big]^{S}
. We conclude that (
3.28
) is an embedding. It follows that the surjective map (
3.25
) is an isomorphism.

Now, both sides of (
3.24
) are graded free
A^{\sigma}(\bar{\mu})\simeq\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(-\vec{\mu})
-modules, see Theorem
2.4
and Lemma
3.20
. Moreover, their fibers at
0
are the twisted local Weyl module
W^{\sigma}(\bar{\mu})
. By comparing their graded characters (as graded
\mathfrak{h}^{\sigma}
-modules), we conclude that (
3.24
) is an isomorphism. ∎

Theorem 3.22
.

Given fundamental coweights
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\in X_{*}(T)^{+}
with
\bar{\lambda}
being the image of
\sum\lambda_{i}
in
X_{*}(T)^{+}_{\sigma}
, there is an isomorphism of
\mathfrak{g}[t]^{\sigma}
-modules

\mathbb{D}^{\sigma}(1,\vec{\lambda})\simeq\mathbb{W}^{\sigma}(\iota(\bar{\lambda})),

and an isomorphism of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(1,\vec{\lambda})\simeq A^{\sigma}(\iota(\bar{\lambda}))
.

Proof.

The theorem directly follows from Theorem
3.21
and Corollary
2.7
.
∎

Remark 3.23
.

From part (1) of Theorem
2.4
and Theorem
3.22
, we are able to determine the algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}(c,\vec{\lambda})
when
c=1
and
\vec{\lambda}
is a collection of fundamental coweights.

4.
Beilinson-Drinfeld Grassmannians of parahoric Bruhat-Tits group schemes

In this section, we define Beilinson-Drinfeld Schubert varieties of certain parahoric Bruhat-Tits group schemes, and we prove their flatness over the base.

4.1.
Beilinson-Drinfeld Grassmannian

Let
G
be a reductive group over
\mathbb{C}
. Let
\Gamma
be a finite group acting on
G
which preserves a maximal torus
T
. Let
C
be an irreducible smooth algebraic curve over
\mathbb{C}
with a faithful
\Gamma
-action. Denote by
\bar{C}
the quotient curve
C/\Gamma
. Let
\pi:C\to\bar{C}
be the quotient map, and denote by
\bar{p}:=\pi(p)
the image of a point
p\in C
. For each point
p\in C
, denote by
\Gamma_{p}
the stabilizer of
p
in
\Gamma
. We say a point
p\in C
is unramified (resp. ramified) if
\Gamma_{p}
is trivial (resp. nontrivial).

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
be irreducible smooth curves over
\mathbb{C}
with faithful
\Gamma
-actions. Let
f:C_{1}\to C_{2}
be a
\Gamma
-equivariant étale morphism such that
\Gamma_{p}=\Gamma_{f(p)}
for any
p\in C_{1}
. Then, the induced map
\bar{f}:\bar{C}_{1}\to\bar{C}_{2}
is also étale, and there is an isomorphism

(4.1)

C_{1}\simeq C_{2}\times_{\bar{C}_{2}}\bar{C}_{1}.

Proof.

We can assume
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
are affine curves. We denote by
\pi_{i}:C_{i}\to\bar{C}_{i}
the projection maps for
i=1,2
.
Let
K_{1}
,
K_{2}
be their function fields, and
\mathscr{O}_{1}
,
\mathscr{O}_{2}
be the coordinate rings. Then we have a diagram of field extensions

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 10.17113pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-9.84723pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{K_{1}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 34.49503pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces K_{2}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.84723pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-10.17113pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{K_{1}^{\sigma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-7.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 34.17113pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces K_{2}^{\sigma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.17114pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 44.34225pt\raise-7.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Observing that
K_{2}\cap K_{1}^{\sigma}=K_{2}^{\sigma}
, we have

[K_{2}K_{1}^{\sigma}:K_{2}^{\sigma}]=[K_{2}K_{1}^{\sigma}:K_{2}][K_{2}:K_{2}^{\sigma}]=[K_{1}^{\sigma}:K_{2}^{\sigma}][K_{2}:K_{2}^{\sigma}]=[K_{1}:K_{2}^{\sigma}].

Thus,
K_{1}=K_{2}K_{1}^{\sigma}
. Since
K_{1}/K_{1}^{\sigma}
is finite, we have
K_{1}=K_{1}^{\sigma}(\theta)
for some
\theta\in K_{1}
, and
f(\theta)=0
for some polynomial
f
over
K_{1}^{\sigma}
. By multiplying elements in
\mathscr{O}^{\sigma}_{1}
, all coefficients of
f
can be made to be in
\mathscr{O}^{\sigma}_{1}
. Moreover, by modifying
\theta
, we can further assume
f
is monic and
f(\theta)=0
. Thus,
\theta
is integral over
\mathscr{O}_{1}^{\sigma}
. It follows that
\theta
is integral over
\mathscr{O}_{1}
. Since
\mathscr{O}_{1}
is integrally closed in
K_{1}
, we must have
\theta\in\mathscr{O}_{1}
. Hence,
K_{1}\simeq K^{\sigma}_{1}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{1}^{\sigma}}\mathscr{O}_{1}
, since
\mathscr{O}_{1}
is a free
\mathscr{O}_{1}^{\sigma}
-module of rank
[K_{1}:K_{1}^{\sigma}]
. Similarly, we have
K_{2}=K^{\sigma}_{2}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{2}^{\sigma}}\mathscr{O}_{2}
. Therefore, we have

\mathscr{O}_{2}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{2}^{\sigma}}K_{1}^{\sigma}\simeq\mathscr{O}_{2}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{2}^{\sigma}}\mathscr{O}_{1}^{\sigma}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{1}^{\sigma}}K_{1}^{\sigma}\simeq K_{1}\simeq\mathscr{O}_{1}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{1}^{\sigma}}K_{1}^{\sigma}.

By assumption,
\Gamma_{p}=\Gamma_{f(p)}
for any
p\in C_{1}
. The map
f
induces an isomorphism
\mathbb{D}_{p}\simeq\mathbb{D}_{f(p)}
of formal discs around
p
and
f(p)
, for any
p\in C_{1}
. Thus,
f
induces an étale map
\bar{f}:\bar{C}_{1}\to\bar{C}_{2}
. It follows that
C_{2}\times_{\bar{C}_{2}}\bar{C}_{1}
is smooth. For any point
(p,q)\in C_{2}\times_{\bar{C}_{2}}\bar{C}_{1}
, we have
\pi_{2}(p)=\bar{f}(q)
. We pick a point
x
in
C_{1}
such that
\pi_{1}(x)=q
. Then
\pi_{2}(f(x))=\pi_{2}(p)
. Thus,
f(x)=\gamma(p)
for some
\gamma\in\Gamma
. Set
x^{\prime}=\gamma^{-1}(x)
. Then,
f(x^{\prime})=p
and
\pi_{1}(x^{\prime})=q
. This shows the surjectivity of the map
C_{1}\to C_{2}\times_{\bar{C}_{2}}\bar{C}_{1}
. Since
C_{2}\times_{\bar{C}_{2}}\bar{C}_{1}
is reduced and
\mathscr{O}_{2}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{2}^{\sigma}}K_{1}^{\sigma}\simeq K_{1}
, the curve
C_{2}\times_{\bar{C}_{2}}\bar{C}_{1}
is integral and has the same function field as
C_{1}
. Therefore, we have an isomorphism
C_{1}\simeq C_{2}\times_{\bar{C}_{2}}\bar{C}_{1}
.
∎

Let
\mathcal{G}_{\bar{C}}
denote the
\Gamma
-fixed point subgroup scheme
\mathrm{Res}_{C/\bar{C}}\big(G\times C\big)^{\Gamma}
of the Weil restriction
\mathrm{Res}_{C/\bar{C}}\big(G\times C\big)
. We will simply denote it by
\mathcal{G}
if there is no confusion. Now, we prove an étale base change property of
\mathcal{G}
which will be used in Section
4.4
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Under the same setup as in Lemma
4.1
and let
\Gamma
act on
G
. We have an isomorphism of group schemes over
\bar{C}_{1}

\mathcal{G}_{\bar{C}_{1}}\simeq\mathcal{G}_{\bar{C}_{2}}\times_{\bar{C}_{2}}\bar{C}_{1}.

Proof.

Given any scheme
\varphi:S\to\bar{C}_{1}
over
\bar{C}_{1}
, by definition
\mathcal{G}_{\bar{C}_{1}}(S)=\mathrm{Map}^{\Gamma}(C_{1}\times_{\bar{C}_{1}}S,G)
consists of
\Gamma
-equivariant morphisms from
C_{1}\times_{\bar{C}_{1}}S
to
G
.
On the other hand,
(\mathcal{G}_{\bar{C}_{2}}\times_{\bar{C}_{2}}\bar{C}_{1})(S)
consists of
\Gamma
-equivariant morphisms from
C_{2}\times_{\bar{C}_{2}}S
to
G
, where
S
is regarded as a scheme over
\bar{C}_{2}
via
f\circ\varphi:S\to\bar{C}_{2}
. By Lemma
4.1
, we have an isomorphism

C_{1}\times_{\bar{C}_{1}}S\simeq(C_{2}\times_{\bar{C}_{2}}\bar{C}_{1})\times_{\bar{C}_{1}}S\simeq C_{2}\times_{\bar{C}_{2}}S.

Thus,
\mathcal{G}_{\bar{C}_{1}}(S)\simeq(\mathcal{G}_{\bar{C}_{2}}\times_{\bar{C}_{2}}\bar{C}_{1})(S)
for any scheme
S
over
\bar{C}_{1}
.
∎

For any
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
R
, and any morphism
p:\mathrm{Spec}(R)\to C
, we denote by
\Delta_{p}\subseteq C\times\mathrm{Spec}(R)
the graph of
p
. Let
C_{R}:=C\times\mathrm{Spec}(R)
. We denote by
\hat{\Delta}_{p}
the formal completion of
C_{R}
along
\Delta_{p}
, and by
\hat{\Delta}_{p}^{*}
the punctured formal completion along
\Delta_{p}
, cf.
[
21
, Chapter II Section 9]
. By
[
35
,
Tag 0GBA
,
Tag 0AMC
]
, we have an equivalent definition of the formal completion
\hat{D}
of
C_{R}
along a closed subscheme
D
as the following functor, for any
R
-scheme
u:T\to\mathrm{Spec}(R)
,

(4.2)

\hat{D}(T)=\{\phi:T\to C\,|\,(\phi,u)\circ\alpha\in D(T_{\mathrm{red}})\},

where
T_{\mathrm{red}}
is the reduced scheme of
T
with the natural embedding
\alpha:T_{\mathrm{red}}\to T
.

We recall the definition of Beilinson-Drinfeld Grassmannians of general group schemes.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
X
be a smooth algebraic curve over
\mathbb{C}
. Let
\mathcal{G}
be a smooth affine group scheme over
X
. The Beilinson-Drinfeld Grassmannian
\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{G},C^{n}}
and the jet group scheme
L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{X^{n}}
over
X^{n}
are defined as follows: for any
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
R
, define

\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{G},X^{n}}(R):=\left\{(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n},\mathcal{F},\beta)\,\middle|\,p_{i}\in X(R),\mathcal{F}\text{ a }\mathcal{G}\text{-torsor on }X_{R},\beta:\mathcal{F}|_{X_{R}\setminus\cup\Delta_{p_{i}}}\simeq\mathring{\mathcal{F}}|_{X_{R}\setminus\cup\Delta_{p_{i}}}\right\}

and

L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{X^{n}}(R):=\left\{(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n},\eta)\,\middle|\,p_{i}\in X(R),\eta:\mathring{\mathcal{F}}|_{\widehat{\cup{\Delta}_{p_{i}}}}\simeq\mathring{\mathcal{F}}|_{\widehat{\cup{\Delta}_{p_{i}}}}\right\},

where
\mathring{\mathcal{F}}
is the trivial
\mathcal{G}
-torsor on
X_{R}
, and
\widehat{\cup{\Delta}_{p_{i}}}
is the completion of
X_{R}
along
{\cup{\Delta}_{p_{i}}}
.

There is a left
L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{X^{n}}
-action on
\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{G},X^{n}}
given by the following map

(4.3)

\displaystyle L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{X^{n}}\times\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{G},X^{n}}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{G},X^{n}}

\displaystyle\big((\vec{p},\eta),(\vec{p},\mathcal{F},\beta)\big)

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(\vec{p},\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\big),

where by Beauville-Laszlo’s Lemma, there exists a unique
\mathcal{G}
-torsor
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
(up to a unique isomorphism) with isomorphisms
\delta:{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}|_{\widehat{\cup\Delta_{p_{i}}}}\simeq\mathcal{F}|_{\widehat{\cup\Delta_{p_{i}}}}
and
\beta^{\prime}:\mathcal{F}^{\prime}|_{C_{R}\setminus\cup\Delta_{p_{i}}}\simeq\mathring{\mathcal{F}}|_{C_{R}\setminus\cup\Delta_{p_{i}}}
such that
\beta^{\prime}\circ\delta^{-1}|_{{\widehat{\cup\Delta_{p_{i}}}}^{*}}=\eta\circ\beta|_{{\widehat{\cup\Delta_{p_{i}}}}^{*}}
. Here
{\widehat{\cup\Delta_{p_{i}}}}^{*}:=\big({{\widehat{\cup\Delta_{p_{i}}}}}\big)\cap\big(X_{R}\setminus\cup\Delta_{p_{i}}\big)
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be an étale morphism of irreducible smooth curves. Let
\mathcal{G}_{Y}
be a smooth affine group scheme over
Y
, and let
\mathcal{G}_{X}=\mathcal{G}_{Y}\times_{Y}X
. Let
Z=\{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in X^{n}\,|\,x_{i}\neq x_{j},f(x_{i})=f(x_{j})\text{ for some }i\neq j\}
. We have an isomorphism

\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{X},X^{n}\setminus Z}\simeq\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{Y},Y^{n}}\times_{Y^{n}}(X^{n}\setminus Z).

Proof.

Let
S
be a scheme over
\mathbb{C}
. When
n=2
, an
S
-point
(x_{1},x_{2})\in(X^{2}\setminus Z)(S)
is a pair of points
x_{1},x_{2}\in X(S)
such that the equalizer
\mathrm{Eq}(x_{1},x_{2})
of
x_{1},x_{2}:S\to X
is naturally isomorphic to the equalizer
\mathrm{Eq}(y_{1},y_{2})
of
y_{1},y_{2}:S\to Y
, where
y_{i}=f\circ x_{i}\in Y(S)
. We shall show
{f}_{S}=f\times\mathrm{Id}_{S}:X_{S}=X\times S\to Y\times S=Y_{S}
induces an isomorphism
\widehat{\Delta_{x_{1}}\cup\Delta_{x_{2}}}\simeq\widehat{\Delta_{y_{1}}\cup\Delta_{y_{2}}}
. Firstly,
f_{S}
is étale and restricts to isomorphisms
\Gamma_{x_{1}}\simeq\Delta_{y_{1}}
and
\Delta_{x_{2}}\simeq\Delta_{y_{2}}
. Since
\mathrm{Eq}(x_{1},x_{2})\simeq\mathrm{Eq}(y_{1},y_{2})
, we have an isomorphism

\Delta_{x_{1}}\times_{X_{S}}\Delta_{x_{2}}\simeq\mathrm{Eq}\big(({x}_{1}\times\mathrm{Id}_{S}),({x}_{2}\times\mathrm{Id}_{S})\big)\simeq\mathrm{Eq}\big(({y}_{1}\times\mathrm{Id}_{S}),({y}_{2}\times\mathrm{Id}_{S})\big)\simeq\Delta_{y_{1}}\times_{Y_{S}}\Delta_{y_{2}}.

Thus,
{f}_{S}
induces an isomorphism

(4.4)

{\Delta_{x_{1}}\cup\Delta_{x_{2}}}\simeq{\Delta_{y_{1}}\cup\Delta_{y_{2}}}.

For any
S
-scheme
u:T\to S
, by (
4.2
), we have

(4.5)

\widehat{\Delta_{x_{1}}\cup\Delta_{x_{2}}}(T)=\{\phi:T\to X\mid(\phi,u)\circ\alpha\in(\Delta_{x_{1}}\cup\Delta_{x_{2}})(T_{\mathrm{red}})\},

where
T_{\mathrm{red}}
is the reduced scheme of
T
with the natural embedding
\alpha:T_{\mathrm{red}}\to T
. Then,
we have a map
\iota:\widehat{\Delta_{x_{1}}\cup\Delta_{x_{2}}}(T)\to\widehat{\Delta_{y_{1}}\cup\Delta_{y_{2}}}(T)
given by
\phi\mapsto f\circ\phi
. By Lemma
4.6
,
\iota
is bijective. Thus, we have the following isomorphisms

(4.6)

\widehat{\Delta_{x_{1}}\cup\Delta_{x_{2}}}\simeq\widehat{\Delta_{y_{1}}\cup\Delta_{y_{2}}},\quad\widehat{\Delta_{x_{1}}\cup\Delta_{x_{2}}}^{*}\simeq\widehat{\Delta_{y_{1}}\cup\Delta_{y_{2}}}^{*}

where
∗
represents the punctured formal completion. Let
\mathcal{F}
be a
\mathcal{G}_{X}
-torsor over
X_{S}
with a trivialization
\beta:\mathcal{F}|_{X_{S}\setminus(\Delta_{x_{1}}\cup\Delta_{x_{2}})}\simeq\mathcal{F}^{0}|_{X_{S}\setminus(\Delta_{x_{1}}\cup\Delta_{x_{2}})}
. By Beaville-Laszlo lemma, this is equivalent to the data consisting of a torsor
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
over
\widehat{\Delta_{x_{1}}\cup\Delta_{x_{2}}}
and a trivialization
\beta^{\prime}
over
\widehat{\Delta_{x_{1}}\cup\Delta_{x_{2}}}^{*}
. By isomorphisms in (
4.6
), we get a torsor
\mathcal{P}
over
\widehat{\Delta_{y_{1}}\cup\Delta_{y_{2}}}
and a trivialization
\eta
over
\widehat{\Delta_{y_{1}}\cup\Delta_{y_{2}}}^{*}
. Again, this is equivalent to the data of a
\mathcal{G}_{Y}
-torsor over
Y_{S}
with a trivialization over
Y_{S}\setminus(\Delta_{y_{1}}\cup\Delta_{y_{2}})
, which will still be denoted by
\mathcal{P}
and
\eta
. Thus, we get an isomorphism

\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{X},X^{2}\setminus Z}\to\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{Y},Y^{2}}\times_{X^{2}}(X^{2}\setminus Z),

given by
(x_{1},x_{2},\mathcal{F},\beta)\mapsto\big((y_{1},y_{2},\mathcal{P},\eta),(x_{1},x_{2})\big)
.

When
n\geq 3
, by induction on
n
, we assume that
f_{S}
induces an isomorphism

\Delta_{x_{1}}\cup\cdots\cup\Delta_{x_{n-1}}\simeq\Delta_{y_{1}}\cup\cdots\cup\Delta_{y_{n-1}}.

Then,
(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n-1}\Delta_{x_{i}})\times_{X_{S}}\Delta_{x_{n}}\simeq\bigcup_{i=1}^{n-1}(\Delta_{x_{i}}\times_{X_{S}}\Delta_{x_{n}})\simeq\bigcup_{i=1}^{n-1}(\Delta_{y_{i}}\times_{Y_{S}}\Delta_{y_{n}})\simeq(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n-1}\Delta_{y_{i}})\times_{Y_{S}}\Delta_{y_{n}}
. Thus,
f_{S}
induces an isomorphism
\Delta_{x_{1}}\cup\cdots\cup\Delta_{x_{n}}\simeq\Delta_{y_{1}}\cup\cdots\cup\Delta_{y_{n}}
. By the same argument as in the case when
n=2
, we have isomorphisms
\widehat{\bigcup_{i}\Delta_{x_{i}}}\simeq\widehat{\bigcup_{i}\Delta_{y_{i}}}
,
\widehat{\bigcup_{i}\Delta_{x_{i}}}^{*}\simeq\widehat{\bigcup_{i}\Delta_{y_{i}}}^{*}
, and

\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{X},X^{n}\setminus Z}\simeq\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{Y},Y^{n}}\times_{Y^{n}}(X^{n}\setminus Z).\qed

Remark 4.5
.

When
n=1
,
Z
is the empty set. In this case, Proposition
4.4
is exactly
[
38
, Lemma 3.2]
. When
n\geq 2
, a statement was formulated incorrectly in
[
39
, Prop.3.1.20]
.

Lemma 4.6
.

Suppose we are in the same setup and assumptions as in Proposition
4.4
and its proof.
For any
\psi\in Y(T)
and
u:T\to S
such that
(\psi\circ\alpha,u\circ\alpha)\in(\Delta_{y_{1}}\cup\Delta_{y_{2}})(T_{\rm red})
,
we have a unique lifting
\phi\in X(T)
such that the following diagram commutes

(4.7)

and
(\phi\circ\alpha,u\circ\alpha)\in(\Delta_{x_{1}}\cup\Delta_{x_{2}})(T_{\rm red})
.

Proof.

We may assume that
S=\mathrm{Spec}(A)
and
T=\mathrm{Spec}(B)
for some commutative rings
A,B
over
\mathbb{C}
. By assumption
(\psi\circ\alpha,u\circ\alpha)\in(\Delta_{y_{1}}\cup\Delta_{y_{2}})(T_{\rm red})
. Then the isomorphism (
4.4
) induces a morphism
(\phi_{\rm red},u\circ\alpha):T_{\rm red}\to X\times S
such that
f\circ\phi_{\rm red}=\psi\circ\alpha
and
(\phi_{\rm red},u\circ\alpha)\in(\Delta_{x_{1}}\cup\Delta_{x_{2}})(T_{\rm red})
. By
[
35
,
Tag 02HF
]
,
f
is étale implies that
f
is formally étale. When
B
is Noetherian, its Jacobson ideal
J(B)
is nilpotent, i.e. there exists an integer
k
such that
J(B)^{k}=0
. Then, by the definition of formally étale morphism, there exists a unique morphism
\phi\in X(T)
such that
\phi\circ\alpha=\phi_{\rm red}
and

f\circ\phi=\psi
.

We now consider the general case. Let
h:A\to B
be the ring homomorphism induced from
u:T\to S
. Since
X
is of finite type, there exists a finitely generated
\mathbb{C}
-subalgebra
A^{\prime}
of
A
and
x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2}\in X(A^{\prime})
such that
p(x^{\prime}_{i})=x_{i}
, i=1,2, where
p:S\to S^{\prime}:=\mathrm{Spec}(A^{\prime})
is induced from
A^{\prime}\to A
, see the left diagram in (
4.8
).

(4.8)

\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x_{i}}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{\exists\,S^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x_{i}^{\prime}}

\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\scriptstyle{(\psi,u)}
\textstyle{Y\times S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Id}_{Y}\times p}
\textstyle{\exists\,T^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\psi^{\prime},u^{\prime})}
\textstyle{Y\times S^{\prime}}

For any
\psi\in Y(T)=Y(B)
such that
(\psi,u)\circ\alpha\in(\Delta_{y_{1}}\cup\Delta_{y_{2}})(T_{\rm red})
, there exists a finitely generated
\mathbb{C}
-subalgebra
B^{\prime}
of
B
and
\psi^{\prime}\in Y(B^{\prime})
such that
h(A^{\prime})\subset B^{\prime}
,
and
q(\psi^{\prime})=\psi
, where
q:T\to T^{\prime}
is induced from
B^{\prime}\subset B
. Let
u^{\prime}:T^{\prime}\to S^{\prime}
be the morphism associated to
h:A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}
. Then, we have the commutative diagram on the right side of (
4.8
). Since
S^{\prime}
and
T^{\prime}
are Noetherian, there exists a unique
\phi^{\prime}\in X(S^{\prime})
such that
f\circ\phi^{\prime}=\psi^{\prime}
and
(\phi^{\prime},u^{\prime})\circ\alpha^{\prime}\in(\Delta_{x^{\prime}_{1}}\cup\Delta_{x^{\prime}_{2}})(T^{\prime}_{\rm red})
, where
\alpha^{\prime}:T^{\prime}_{\rm red}\to T^{\prime}
is associated to the quotient morphism
B^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}/J(B^{\prime})
with
J(B^{\prime})
being the Jacobson ideal of
B^{\prime}
. Thus, we have the following comutative diagram.

(4.9)

where
q_{\rm red}:T^{\prime}_{\rm red}\to T_{\rm red}
is the induced from
q:T^{\prime}\to T
.
Set
\phi=\phi^{\prime}\circ q
. We shall show that
\phi
is the desired lifting.

It is clear that
f\circ\phi=\psi
. Note that the following diagram commutes,

(4.10)

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 42.46832pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-12.41496pt\raise
